Specialist AI: JAH Presolved Physics Derivations Librarian (Signature)

JAH Presolved Physics Derivations Librarian (Signature) answers questions about this database, explains any record in plain sentences, compares records side by side on the same fields, and helps a reader follow the records in order. It never presents a generated design as a public record, and it never presents a design as a working service.

Boundless generator

The generator is seeded and deterministic, throttled to at most 100 records per call, duplicate-checked by deterministic hash ids, and hard-stopped at 1,000,000,000 records. Generator ceiling: up to 1,000,000,000 scales per file, generated on demand by the file AI — not stored files. Every generated record is labeled src "generated". Open the page script to use window.PresolvedPhysicsDerivationsSignatureTools.

Validation engine

Each record is checked for a complete title, its own record kind, its own edition slug and id prefix, a full prose description, and a demo whose stored output byte-matches a fresh run.

Derive v = u + at (Signature variant): u = 10 m/s, a = 3 m/s², t = 4 s JAH-PSPDS-000001 · simple

Evidence: design-study Signature-made variant of the same kind of problem, labelled design-study and never presented as a public fact. TESTED: the answer is recomputed by the demo from the stated inputs and compared with the stated value (PASS).

This record presolves the physics derivation 'Derive v = u + at (Signature variant): u = 10 m/s, a = 3 m/s², t = 4 s'. It starts from the stated first principles, carries the derivation through every algebraic step with nothing skipped, states the final formula, and then checks that formula against a worked numerical example. A derivation is the purest presolved shape: the answer is not a recalled fact but a result proved from principles, and the record keeps the proof and the check side by side so that an AI retrieving it gets both the reasoning and the evidence. The runnable demo re-performs the derivation's numerical check in code, so the final formula is verified by execution as well as by algebra.

History

Derivations of this form are the backbone of classical and modern physics as taught in the standard university texts, above all the OpenStax University Physics sequence and the treatments collected in HyperPhysics. Each one begins from a definition or a conservation law and ends at a formula that working physicists and engineers use daily. This instance, 'Derive v = u + at (Signature variant): u = 10 m/s, a = 3 m/s², t = 4 s', is derived here from first principles with fixed numerical values for the check, so that every step can be followed and the final formula tested against a concrete case.

Description

This record presolves the physics derivation 'Derive v = u + at (Signature variant): u = 10 m/s, a = 3 m/s², t = 4 s'. It starts from the stated first principles, carries the derivation through every algebraic step with nothing skipped, states the final formula, and then checks that formula against a worked numerical example. A derivation is the purest presolved shape: the answer is not a recalled fact but a result proved from principles, and the record keeps the proof and the check side by side so that an AI retrieving it gets both the reasoning and the evidence. The runnable demo re-performs the derivation's numerical check in code, so the final formula is verified by execution as well as by algebra.

The Problem

Derive the relation v = u + at from the definition of acceleration, and check it numerically for u = 10 m/s, a = 3 m/s², t = 4 s.

The Worked Solution

The solution proceeds in the following steps. Step 1: Acceleration is defined as the rate of change of velocity: a = (v − u)/t, where u is the initial velocity and v is the velocity after time t. Step 2: Multiply both sides by t: a×t = v − u. Step 3: Add u to both sides: v = u + at. This is the first equation of motion, valid for constant acceleration. Step 4: Numerical check: v = 10 + 3 × 4 = 10 + 12 = 22 m/s. Step 5: Cross-check: (v − u)/t = (22 − 10)/4 = 3 m/s², which recovers the stated acceleration. That completes the worked solution; the answer is v = 22 m/s.

The Answer

Derived: v = u + at. Numerical check: with u = 10 m/s, a = 3 m/s² and t = 4 s, v = 22 m/s. That value is the numerical check of the derived formula for the stated inputs; the formula itself is the derivation's answer, and the number shows the formula working on a concrete case.

When It Applies

This record applies whenever the stated problem is the problem in front of you: the same question, the same configuration, the same stated inputs. The solution is checked in three ways. First, the worked solution shows every step, so a reader can follow the arithmetic or the table row by row and find any slip. Second, the demo recomputes the answer from the problem's own inputs in code and prints the result together with a SHA-256 digest of the computed payload, run twice, with a Deterministic: MATCH line confirming the two runs agree; the demo also compares the computed answer with the stated value and prints PASS when they agree. Third, the program repeats the same computation as a reusable routine, so the answer can be regenerated on demand. As a Signature design-study record, the verification is the execution itself: the problem is newly composed, and its solution is verified by the demo's recomputation.

Limits

The answer holds only inside its stated conditions. The derivation holds only under its stated assumptions: the idealisations named in the solution (constant acceleration, no friction, point masses, ideal gases, and similar) limit where the result applies, and outside those conditions the formula must be corrected or replaced. Beyond those conditions the answer changes or stops applying: different inputs give different answers, and the record's numbers must not be carried into a different configuration without redoing the work. Nothing here is a design approval, a safety certification, or a claim about a physical system that was never measured. The record proves what it states about the stated problem and no more, and any use beyond that problem should repeat the computation with the new data.

Math Proof

The mathematical justification is the chain of algebra from the starting principle to the final formula. v = u + a * t — v is the final velocity (m/s); u is the initial velocity (10 m/s); a is the constant acceleration (3 m/s²); t is the elapsed time (4 s). Each step is a substitution, a rearrangement, or an integration of a stated relation, and the worked solution shows every one. The numerical check then evaluates the final formula for the stated values and obtains v = 22 m/s, which the demo recomputes in code and compares with the stated value. A new Signature instance of the v = u + at derivation, with different starting values from the public record. The derivation and the check agree, which is the verification this record offers.

Science Proof

The derivation is scientifically sound because each step follows from a stated principle or a definition, and no step smuggles in the conclusion. A new Signature instance of the v = u + at derivation, with different starting values from the public record. The final formula is then tested numerically: the demo evaluates it for the stated values and checks it against an independent computation of the same quantity, so an algebraic slip would show up as a failed check. The record states where the derivation's assumptions hold — the idealisations, the ranges, and the regimes where the formula stops applying — so the result is used only inside its domain.

Demo

The demo recomputes the answer from the problem's inputs in code and prints the computed result, a SHA-256 digest of the computed payload run twice, and a Deterministic: MATCH line. Its exact output is stored in expected_output; run it with: python3 build_lane_b.py --demo JAH-PSPDS-000001

Program

The program is a full working routine (class PresolvedSolution with a run() method) that performs the same computation as the demo and prints the same output, so the answer can be regenerated on demand.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
u = 10.0
a = 3.0
t = 4.0
v = u + a * t
_expected_v = 22.0
_check_v = abs(v - _expected_v) <= 0.01
_chk_a_check = (v - u) / t
_exp_a_check = 3.0
_ok_a_check = abs(_chk_a_check - _exp_a_check) <= 0.01
lines = [
    f"u = {u:.2f} m/s",
    f"a = {a:.2f} m/s^2",
    f"t = {t:.2f} s",
    f"v = {v:.2f} m/s",
    f"Check v against stated value {_expected_v:.2f} m/s: {'PASS' if _check_v else 'FAIL'}",
    f"Check a_check: {'PASS' if _ok_a_check else 'FAIL'} (computed {_chk_a_check:.2f}, expected {_exp_a_check:.2f})",
]
payload = f"v={v:.6g}; a_check={_chk_a_check:.6g}"
for _line in lines:
    print(_line)
_h1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
_h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {_h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {_h2}")
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if _h1 == _h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

Full working program

import hashlib


class PresolvedSolution:
    """Derive v = u + at (Signature variant): u = 10 m/s, a = 3 m/s², t = 4 s — presolved solution. run() recomputes the answer from the stated inputs."""

    def run(self):
        import math
        u = 10.0
        a = 3.0
        t = 4.0
        v = u + a * t
        _expected_v = 22.0
        _check_v = abs(v - _expected_v) <= 0.01
        _chk_a_check = (v - u) / t
        _exp_a_check = 3.0
        _ok_a_check = abs(_chk_a_check - _exp_a_check) <= 0.01
        lines = [
            f"u = {u:.2f} m/s",
            f"a = {a:.2f} m/s^2",
            f"t = {t:.2f} s",
            f"v = {v:.2f} m/s",
            f"Check v against stated value {_expected_v:.2f} m/s: {'PASS' if _check_v else 'FAIL'}",
            f"Check a_check: {'PASS' if _ok_a_check else 'FAIL'} (computed {_chk_a_check:.2f}, expected {_exp_a_check:.2f})",
        ]
        payload = f"v={v:.6g}; a_check={_chk_a_check:.6g}"
        return lines, payload


if __name__ == "__main__":
    _solution = PresolvedSolution()
    _lines, _payload = _solution.run()
    for _line in _lines:
        print(_line)
    _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {_h1}")
    print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {_h2}")
    print("Deterministic: MATCH" if _h1 == _h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

The Problem

Derive the relation s = ut + ½at² for motion under constant acceleration, and check it numerically for u = 10 m/s, a = 3 m/s², t = 4 s.

The Worked Solution

The solution proceeds in the following steps. Step 1: Under constant acceleration the velocity grows linearly from u to v = u + at. Step 2: The average velocity over the interval is (u + v)/2 = (u + u + at)/2 = u + at/2. Step 3: Displacement is average velocity times time: s = (u + at/2) × t. Step 4: Expanding gives s = ut + ½at², the second equation of motion. Step 5: Numerical check: s = 10 × 4 + 0.5 × 3 × 16 = 40 + 24 = 64 m; by average velocity, (10 + 22)/2 × 4 = 64 m, agreeing. That completes the worked solution; the answer is s = 64 m.

The Answer

Derived: s = ut + ½at². Numerical check: with u = 10 m/s, a = 3 m/s² and t = 4 s, s = 64 m. That value is the numerical check of the derived formula for the stated inputs; the formula itself is the derivation's answer, and the number shows the formula working on a concrete case.

When It Applies

This record applies whenever the stated problem is the problem in front of you: the same question, the same configuration, the same stated inputs. The solution is checked in three ways. First, the worked solution shows every step, so a reader can follow the arithmetic or the table row by row and find any slip. Second, the demo recomputes the answer from the problem's own inputs in code and prints the result together with a SHA-256 digest of the computed payload, run twice, with a Deterministic: MATCH line confirming the two runs agree; the demo also compares the computed answer with the stated value and prints PASS when they agree. Third, the program repeats the same computation as a reusable routine, so the answer can be regenerated on demand. As a Signature design-study record, the verification is the execution itself: the problem is newly composed, and its solution is verified by the demo's recomputation.

Each step is a substitution, a rearrangement, or an integration of a stated relation, and the worked solution shows every one. import hashlib
import math
u = 10.0
a = 3.0

s = u * t + 0.5 * a * t ** 2
_expected_s = 64.0
_check_s = abs(s - _expected_s) <= 0.01
_chk_s_avg = (u + v) / 2.0 * t
_exp_s_avg = 64.0
_ok_s_avg = abs(_chk_s_avg - _exp_s_avg) <= 0.01
lines = [
    f"u = {u:.2f} m/s",
    f"a = {a:.2f} m/s^2",
    f"t = {t:.2f} s",
    f"v = {v:.2f} m/s",
    f"Check v against stated value {_expected_v:.2f} m/s: {'PASS' if _check_v else 'FAIL'}",
    f"s = {s:.2f} m",
    f"Check s against stated value {_expected_s:.2f} m: {'PASS' if _check_s else 'FAIL'}",
    f"Check s_avg: {'PASS' if _ok_s_avg else 'FAIL'} (computed {_chk_s_avg:.2f}, expected {_exp_s_avg:.2f})",
]

    print(_line)

print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {_h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {_h2}")
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if _h1 == _h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

import hashlib


class PresolvedSolution:
    """Derive s = ut + ½at² (Signature variant): u = 10 m/s, a = 3 m/s², t = 4 s — presolved solution. run() recomputes the answer from the stated inputs."""

    def run(self):
        import math
        u = 10.0
        a = 3.0
        t = 4.0
        v = u + a * t
        _expected_v = 22.0

        _expected_s = 64.0
        _check_s = abs(s - _expected_s) <= 0.01

        lines = [
            f"u = {u:.2f} m/s",
            f"a = {a:.2f} m/s^2",
            f"t = {t:.2f} s",
            f"v = {v:.2f} m/s",
            f"Check v against stated value {_expected_v:.2f} m/s: {'PASS' if _check_v else 'FAIL'}",
            f"s = {s:.2f} m",
            f"Check s against stated value {_expected_s:.2f} m: {'PASS' if _check_s else 'FAIL'}",
            f"Check s_avg: {'PASS' if _ok_s_avg else 'FAIL'} (computed {_chk_s_avg:.2f}, expected {_exp_s_avg:.2f})",
        ]
        payload = f"v={v:.6g}; s={s:.6g}; s_avg={_chk_s_avg:.6g}"
        return lines, payload


if __name__ == "__main__":

This record presolves the physics derivation 'Derive v² = u² + 2as (Signature variant): u = 10 m/s, a = 3 m/s², s = 64 m'. It starts from the stated first principles, carries the derivation through every algebraic step with nothing skipped, states the final formula, and then checks that formula against a worked numerical example. The Problem

Derive the relation v² = u² + 2as for motion under constant acceleration, and check it numerically for u = 10 m/s, a = 3 m/s², s = 64 m.

The Worked Solution

The solution proceeds in the following steps. Step 1: Start from v = u + at and s = ut + ½at². Step 2: Solve the first for t: t = (v − u)/a. Step 3: Substitute into the second: s = u(v − u)/a + a(v − u)²/(2a²). Step 4: Multiply through by 2a and simplify: 2as = 2u(v − u) + (v − u)² = v² − u². Step 5: Rearranging gives v² = u² + 2as. Numerical check: v² = 100 + 2 × 3 × 64 = 484, so v = 22 m/s. That completes the worked solution; the answer is v = 22 m/s.

The Answer

Derived: v² = u² + 2as. Numerical check: with u = 10 m/s, a = 3 m/s² and s = 64 m, v² = 484, so v = 22 m/s. That value is the numerical check of the derived formula for the stated inputs; the formula itself is the derivation's answer, and the number shows the formula working on a concrete case.

When It Applies

This record applies whenever the stated problem is the problem in front of you: the same question, the same configuration, the same stated inputs. The solution is checked in three ways. First, the worked solution shows every step, so a reader can follow the arithmetic or the table row by row and find any slip. Second, the demo recomputes the answer from the problem's own inputs in code and prints the result together with a SHA-256 digest of the computed payload, run twice, with a Deterministic: MATCH line confirming the two runs agree; the demo also compares the computed answer with the stated value and prints PASS when they agree. Third, the program repeats the same computation as a reusable routine, so the answer can be regenerated on demand. As a Signature design-study record, the verification is the execution itself: the problem is newly composed, and its solution is verified by the demo's recomputation.

Beyond those conditions the answer changes or stops applying: different inputs give different answers, and the record's numbers must not be carried into a different configuration without redoing the work. Nothing here is a design approval, a safety certification, or a claim about a physical system that was never measured. The record proves what it states about the stated problem and no more, and any use beyond that problem should repeat the computation with the new data.

The Problem

Derive F = ma from the definition of momentum and Newton's second law, and check it numerically for m = 5 kg, a velocity change of 8 m/s in 4 s.

The Worked Solution

The solution proceeds in the following steps. Step 1: Momentum is p = mv. Newton's second law states that force is the rate of change of momentum: F = Δp/Δt. Step 2: For a constant mass, Δp = m × Δv, so F = m × Δv/Δt. Step 3: But Δv/Δt is the acceleration a, so F = ma. Step 4: Numerical check: a = 8/4 = 2 m/s², so F = 5 × 2 = 10 N. Step 5: Cross-check: Δp = 5 × 8 = 40 kg·m/s, and F = 40/4 = 10 N, agreeing. That completes the worked solution; the answer is F = 10 N.

The Answer

Derived: F = ma. Numerical check: with m = 5 kg and a = 2 m/s², F = 10 N. That value is the numerical check of the derived formula for the stated inputs; the formula itself is the derivation's answer, and the number shows the formula working on a concrete case.

History

Derivations of this form are the backbone of classical and modern physics as taught in the standard university texts, above all the OpenStax University Physics sequence and the treatments collected in HyperPhysics. Each one begins from a definition or a conservation law and ends at a formula that working physicists and engineers use daily. This instance, 'Derive F = ma from momentum (Signature variant): 5 kg, 8 m/s in 4 s', is derived here from first principles with fixed numerical values for the check, so that every step can be followed and the final formula tested against a concrete case.

Problem

Derive F = ma from the definition of momentum and Newton's second law, and check it numerically for m = 5 kg, a velocity change of 8 m/s in 4 s.

Method

The solution proceeds in the following steps. Step 1: Momentum is p = mv. Newton's second law states that force is the rate of change of momentum: F = Δp/Δt. Step 2: For a constant mass, Δp = m × Δv, so F = m × Δv/Δt. Step 3: But Δv/Δt is the acceleration a, so F = ma. Step 4: Numerical check: a = 8/4 = 2 m/s², so F = 5 × 2 = 10 N. Step 5: Cross-check: Δp = 5 × 8 = 40 kg·m/s, and F = 40/4 = 10 N, agreeing. That completes the worked solution; the answer is F = 10 N.

Dataset

Stated numerical values for the check (see key figures); physical constants (g, G, R, h, k, epsilon_0) from the standard references listed in sources.

import hashlib
import math
m = 5.0
delta_v = 8.0
delta_t = 4.0
a = delta_v / delta_t
_expected_a = 2.0
_check_a = abs(a - _expected_a) <= 0.01
F = m * a
_expected_F = 10.0
_check_F = abs(F - _expected_F) <= 0.01
_chk_F_momentum = m * delta_v / delta_t
_exp_F_momentum = 10.0
_ok_F_momentum = abs(_chk_F_momentum - _exp_F_momentum) <= 0.01
lines = [
    f"m = {m:.2f} kg",
    f"delta_v = {delta_v:.2f} m/s",
    f"delta_t = {delta_t:.2f} s",
    f"a = {a:.2f} m/s^2",
    f"Check a against stated value {_expected_a:.2f} m/s^2: {'PASS' if _check_a else 'FAIL'}",
    f"F = {F:.2f} N",
    f"Check F against stated value {_expected_F:.2f} N: {'PASS' if _check_F else 'FAIL'}",
    f"Check F_momentum: {'PASS' if _ok_F_momentum else 'FAIL'} (computed {_chk_F_momentum:.2f}, expected {_exp_F_momentum:.2f})",
]
payload = f"a={a:.6g}; F={F:.6g}; F_momentum={_chk_F_momentum:.6g}"
for _line in lines:
    print(_line)
_h1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
_h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {_h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {_h2}")
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if _h1 == _h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

import hashlib


class PresolvedSolution:
    """Derive F = ma from momentum (Signature variant): 5 kg, 8 m/s in 4 s — presolved solution. run() recomputes the answer from the stated inputs."""

    def run(self):
        import math
        m = 5.0
        delta_v = 8.0
        delta_t = 4.0
        a = delta_v / delta_t
        _expected_a = 2.0
        _check_a = abs(a - _expected_a) <= 0.01
        F = m * a
        _expected_F = 10.0
        _check_F = abs(F - _expected_F) <= 0.01
        _chk_F_momentum = m * delta_v / delta_t
        _exp_F_momentum = 10.0
        _ok_F_momentum = abs(_chk_F_momentum - _exp_F_momentum) <= 0.01
        lines = [
            f"m = {m:.2f} kg",
            f"delta_v = {delta_v:.2f} m/s",
            f"delta_t = {delta_t:.2f} s",
            f"a = {a:.2f} m/s^2",
            f"Check a against stated value {_expected_a:.2f} m/s^2: {'PASS' if _check_a else 'FAIL'}",
            f"F = {F:.2f} N",
            f"Check F against stated value {_expected_F:.2f} N: {'PASS' if _check_F else 'FAIL'}",
            f"Check F_momentum: {'PASS' if _ok_F_momentum else 'FAIL'} (computed {_chk_F_momentum:.2f}, expected {_exp_F_momentum:.2f})",
        ]
        payload = f"a={a:.6g}; F={F:.6g}; F_momentum={_chk_F_momentum:.6g}"
        return lines, payload


if __name__ == "__main__":
    _solution = PresolvedSolution()
    _lines, _payload = _solution.run()
    for _line in _lines:
        print(_line)
    _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {_h1}")
    print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {_h2}")

The Problem

Derive the kinetic energy formula KE = ½mv² from the definition of work, and check it numerically for m = 4 kg, v = 6 m/s.

The Worked Solution

The solution proceeds in the following steps. Step 1: Work is force times distance: W = F × s. For a constant force from rest, F = ma and v² = 2as, so s = v²/(2a). Step 2: Substituting: W = ma × v²/(2a) = ½mv². Step 3: The work done accelerating the mass becomes its kinetic energy: KE = ½mv². Step 4: Numerical check: KE = 0.5 × 4 × 6² = 72 J. Step 5: Cross-check: a force of 24 N acting over 3 m does 72 J of work, agreeing. That completes the worked solution; the answer is KE = 72 J.

The Answer

Derived: KE = ½mv². Numerical check: with m = 4 kg and v = 6 m/s, KE = 72 J. That value is the numerical check of the derived formula for the stated inputs; the formula itself is the derivation's answer, and the number shows the formula working on a concrete case.

Third, the program repeats the same computation as a reusable routine, so the answer can be regenerated on demand. As a Signature design-study record, the verification is the execution itself: the problem is newly composed, and its solution is verified by the demo's recomputation.

The Problem

Derive the gravitational potential energy formula GPE = mgh from the definition of work, and check it numerically for m = 8 kg, h = 12 m (g = 9.81 m/s²).

The Worked Solution

The solution proceeds in the following steps. Step 1: Lifting a mass m through a height h against gravity requires a force equal to its weight, mg. Step 2: Work is force times distance: W = mg × h. Step 3: That work is stored as gravitational potential energy: GPE = mgh, measured relative to the starting height. Step 4: Numerical check: GPE = 8 × 9.81 × 12 = 941.76 J. Step 5: Cross-check: the weight is 78.48 N and the lift is 12 m, giving 78.48 × 12 = 941.76 J, agreeing. That completes the worked solution; the answer is GPE = 941.76 J.

The Answer

Problem

Derive the gravitational potential energy formula GPE = mgh from the definition of work, and check it numerically for m = 8 kg, h = 12 m (g = 9.81 m/s²).

Method

The solution proceeds in the following steps. Step 1: Lifting a mass m through a height h against gravity requires a force equal to its weight, mg. Step 2: Work is force times distance: W = mg × h. Step 3: That work is stored as gravitational potential energy: GPE = mgh, measured relative to the starting height. Step 4: Numerical check: GPE = 8 × 9.81 × 12 = 941.76 J. Step 5: Cross-check: the weight is 78.48 N and the lift is 12 m, giving 78.48 × 12 = 941.76 J, agreeing. That completes the worked solution; the answer is GPE = 941.76 J.

import hashlib
import math
m = 8.0
g = 9.81
h = 12.0
GPE = m * g * h
_expected_GPE = 941.76
_check_GPE = abs(GPE - _expected_GPE) <= 0.01
_chk_W_lift = m * g * h
_exp_W_lift = 941.76
_ok_W_lift = abs(_chk_W_lift - _exp_W_lift) <= 0.01
lines = [
    f"m = {m:.2f} kg",
    f"g = {g:.2f} m/s^2",
    f"h = {h:.2f} m",
    f"GPE = {GPE:.2f} J",
    f"Check GPE against stated value {_expected_GPE:.2f} J: {'PASS' if _check_GPE else 'FAIL'}",
    f"Check W_lift: {'PASS' if _ok_W_lift else 'FAIL'} (computed {_chk_W_lift:.2f}, expected {_exp_W_lift:.2f})",
]
payload = f"GPE={GPE:.6g}; W_lift={_chk_W_lift:.6g}"
for _line in lines:
    print(_line)
_h1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
_h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {_h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {_h2}")
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if _h1 == _h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

This record presolves the physics derivation 'Derive the period of a simple pendulum (Signature variant): L = 2.5 m'. The Problem

Derive the period T = 2π√(L/g) of a simple pendulum, and check it numerically for L = 2.5 m (g = 9.81 m/s²).

The Worked Solution

The solution proceeds in the following steps. Step 1: For small angles the restoring torque on a pendulum of length L is approximately −mgL×θ, where θ is the angular displacement. Step 2: The equation of motion is I×θ-double-dot = −mgL×θ with I = mL², giving θ-double-dot = −(g/L)×θ. Step 3: This is simple harmonic motion with angular frequency ω = √(g/L). Step 4: The period is T = 2π/ω = 2π√(L/g). Step 5: Numerical check: T = 2π × √(2.5/9.81) = 2π × 0.5048 = 3.17 s; inverting, g = 4π²L/T² recovers 9.81 m/s². That completes the worked solution; the answer is T = 3.17 s.

The Answer

Derived: T = 2π√(L/g). Numerical check: with L = 2.5 m and g = 9.81 m/s², T = 3.17 s. That value is the numerical check of the derived formula for the stated inputs; the formula itself is the derivation's answer, and the number shows the formula working on a concrete case.

History

Derivations of this form are the backbone of classical and modern physics as taught in the standard university texts, above all the OpenStax University Physics sequence and the treatments collected in HyperPhysics. Each one begins from a definition or a conservation law and ends at a formula that working physicists and engineers use daily. Description

This record presolves the physics derivation 'Derive centripetal acceleration a = v²/r (Signature variant): 15 m/s, 10 m'. It starts from the stated first principles, carries the derivation through every algebraic step with nothing skipped, states the final formula, and then checks that formula against a worked numerical example. A derivation is the purest presolved shape: the answer is not a recalled fact but a result proved from principles, and the record keeps the proof and the check side by side so that an AI retrieving it gets both the reasoning and the evidence. The runnable demo re-performs the derivation's numerical check in code, so the final formula is verified by execution as well as by algebra.

The Problem

Derive the centripetal acceleration a = v²/r for uniform circular motion, and check it numerically for v = 15 m/s, r = 10 m.

The Worked Solution

The solution proceeds in the following steps. Step 1: In uniform circular motion the velocity vector turns through the same angle as the radius vector, staying perpendicular to it. Step 2: Over a small angle Δθ the change in velocity is Δv = v × Δθ, by similar triangles between the velocity and position diagrams. Step 3: Dividing by Δt and taking the limit gives a = v × ω, where ω = Δθ/Δt is the angular speed. Step 4: Since ω = v/r, the acceleration is a = v²/r, directed towards the centre. Step 5: Numerical check: a = 15²/10 = 22.5 m/s²; by angular speed, ω = 15/10 = 1.5 rad/s and a = ω²r = 2.25 × 10 = 22.5 m/s², agreeing. That completes the worked solution; the answer is a = 22.5 m/s².

The Answer

History

Derivations of this form are the backbone of classical and modern physics as taught in the standard university texts, above all the OpenStax University Physics sequence and the treatments collected in HyperPhysics. Problem

Derive the centripetal acceleration a = v²/r for uniform circular motion, and check it numerically for v = 15 m/s, r = 10 m.

Method

The solution proceeds in the following steps. Step 1: In uniform circular motion the velocity vector turns through the same angle as the radius vector, staying perpendicular to it. Step 2: Over a small angle Δθ the change in velocity is Δv = v × Δθ, by similar triangles between the velocity and position diagrams. Step 3: Dividing by Δt and taking the limit gives a = v × ω, where ω = Δθ/Δt is the angular speed. Step 4: Since ω = v/r, the acceleration is a = v²/r, directed towards the centre. Step 5: Numerical check: a = 15²/10 = 22.5 m/s²; by angular speed, ω = 15/10 = 1.5 rad/s and a = ω²r = 2.25 × 10 = 22.5 m/s², agreeing. That completes the worked solution; the answer is a = 22.5 m/s².

Dataset

Stated numerical values for the check (see key figures); physical constants (g, G, R, h, k, epsilon_0) from the standard references listed in sources.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
v = 15.0
r = 10.0
a = v ** 2 / r
_expected_a = 22.5
_check_a = abs(a - _expected_a) <= 0.01
_chk_a_omega = (v / r) ** 2 * r
_exp_a_omega = 22.5
_ok_a_omega = abs(_chk_a_omega - _exp_a_omega) <= 0.01
lines = [
    f"v = {v:.2f} m/s",
    f"r = {r:.2f} m",
    f"a = {a:.2f} m/s^2",
    f"Check a against stated value {_expected_a:.2f} m/s^2: {'PASS' if _check_a else 'FAIL'}",
    f"Check a_omega: {'PASS' if _ok_a_omega else 'FAIL'} (computed {_chk_a_omega:.2f}, expected {_exp_a_omega:.2f})",
]
payload = f"a={a:.6g}; a_omega={_chk_a_omega:.6g}"
for _line in lines:
    print(_line)
_h1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
_h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {_h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {_h2}")
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if _h1 == _h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

import hashlib


class PresolvedSolution:
    """Derive centripetal acceleration a = v²/r (Signature variant): 15 m/s, 10 m — presolved solution. run() recomputes the answer from the stated inputs."""

    def run(self):
        import math
        v = 15.0
        r = 10.0
        a = v ** 2 / r
        _expected_a = 22.5
        _check_a = abs(a - _expected_a) <= 0.01
        _chk_a_omega = (v / r) ** 2 * r
        _exp_a_omega = 22.5
        _ok_a_omega = abs(_chk_a_omega - _exp_a_omega) <= 0.01
        lines = [
            f"v = {v:.2f} m/s",
            f"r = {r:.2f} m",
            f"a = {a:.2f} m/s^2",
            f"Check a against stated value {_expected_a:.2f} m/s^2: {'PASS' if _check_a else 'FAIL'}",
            f"Check a_omega: {'PASS' if _ok_a_omega else 'FAIL'} (computed {_chk_a_omega:.2f}, expected {_exp_a_omega:.2f})",
        ]
        payload = f"a={a:.6g}; a_omega={_chk_a_omega:.6g}"
        return lines, payload


if __name__ == "__main__":
    _solution = PresolvedSolution()
    _lines, _payload = _solution.run()
    for _line in _lines:
        print(_line)
    _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {_h1}")
    print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {_h2}")
    print("Deterministic: MATCH" if _h1 == _h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

The Problem

Derive the escape velocity v = √(2GM/R) from energy conservation, and check it numerically for the Moon (G = 6.674×10⁻¹¹, M = 7.342×10²² kg, R = 1.737×10⁶ m).

The Worked Solution

The solution proceeds in the following steps. Step 1: A body escapes when its total mechanical energy at infinity is zero: kinetic plus potential energy must sum to zero at the surface. Step 2: Kinetic energy is ½mv² and gravitational potential energy at the surface is −GMm/R. Step 3: Setting the sum to zero: ½mv² = GMm/R. Step 4: Cancelling m and solving gives v = √(2GM/R). Step 5: Numerical check: v = √(2 × 6.674×10⁻¹¹ × 7.342×10²² / 1.737×10⁶) = 2,375 m/s; the kinetic energy per unit mass, ½v², equals GM/R, confirming the energy balance. That completes the worked solution; the answer is v = 2,375 m/s.

The Answer

Derived: v = √(2GM/R). Numerical check: for the Moon, v = 2,375 m/s. That value is the numerical check of the derived formula for the stated inputs; the formula itself is the derivation's answer, and the number shows the formula working on a concrete case.

class PresolvedSolution:
    """Derive escape velocity (Signature variant): the Moon — presolved solution. run() recomputes the answer from the stated inputs."""

        G = 6.674e-11
        M = 7.342e+22
        R = 1737000.0
        v = math.sqrt(2.0 * G * M / R)

        ]
        payload = f"v={v:.6g}; energy_balance={_chk_energy_balance:.6g}"
        return lines, payload

Each one begins from a definition or a conservation law and ends at a formula that working physicists and engineers use daily. This instance, 'Derive Kepler's third law (Signature variant): the orbit of Mars', is derived here from first principles with fixed numerical values for the check, so that every step can be followed and the final formula tested against a concrete case.

Description

This record presolves the physics derivation 'Derive Kepler's third law (Signature variant): the orbit of Mars'. It starts from the stated first principles, carries the derivation through every algebraic step with nothing skipped, states the final formula, and then checks that formula against a worked numerical example. A derivation is the purest presolved shape: the answer is not a recalled fact but a result proved from principles, and the record keeps the proof and the check side by side so that an AI retrieving it gets both the reasoning and the evidence. The runnable demo re-performs the derivation's numerical check in code, so the final formula is verified by execution as well as by algebra.

The Problem

Derive Kepler's third law (T² proportional to a³) from circular orbital motion and Newton's law of gravitation, and check it numerically for the orbit of Mars (T = 5.94×10⁷ s, a = 2.279×10¹¹ m).

The Worked Solution

The solution proceeds in the following steps. Step 1: For a circular orbit, gravity provides the centripetal force: GMm/r² = mv²/r. Step 2: Cancelling m and one factor of r gives v² = GM/r. Step 3: The orbital speed is v = 2πr/T, so (2πr/T)² = GM/r. Step 4: Rearranging gives T² = (4π²/GM) × r³: the square of the period is proportional to the cube of the orbital radius. Step 5: Numerical check: T²/a³ = (5.94×10⁷)²/(2.279×10¹¹)³ = 2.98×10⁻¹⁹ s²/m³, and 4π²/(G×M_sun) gives the same value, confirming the law. That completes the worked solution; the answer is T²/a³ = 2.98×10⁻¹⁹ s²/m³.

The Answer

Derived: T² = (4π²/GM)a³. Numerical check: for Mars, T²/a³ = 2.98×10⁻¹⁹ s²/m³, matching 4π²/(GM_sun). That value is the numerical check of the derived formula for the stated inputs; the formula itself is the derivation's answer, and the number shows the formula working on a concrete case.

When It Applies

This record applies whenever the stated problem is the problem in front of you: the same question, the same configuration, the same stated inputs. The solution is checked in three ways. First, the worked solution shows every step, so a reader can follow the arithmetic or the table row by row and find any slip. Second, the demo recomputes the answer from the problem's own inputs in code and prints the result together with a SHA-256 digest of the computed payload, run twice, with a Deterministic: MATCH line confirming the two runs agree; the demo also compares the computed answer with the stated value and prints PASS when they agree. Third, the program repeats the same computation as a reusable routine, so the answer can be regenerated on demand. As a Signature design-study record, the verification is the execution itself: the problem is newly composed, and its solution is verified by the demo's recomputation.

Limits

The answer holds only inside its stated conditions. The derivation holds only under its stated assumptions: the idealisations named in the solution (constant acceleration, no friction, point masses, ideal gases, and similar) limit where the result applies, and outside those conditions the formula must be corrected or replaced. Beyond those conditions the answer changes or stops applying: different inputs give different answers, and the record's numbers must not be carried into a different configuration without redoing the work. Nothing here is a design approval, a safety certification, or a claim about a physical system that was never measured. The record proves what it states about the stated problem and no more, and any use beyond that problem should repeat the computation with the new data.

Math Proof

The mathematical justification is the chain of algebra from the starting principle to the final formula. T^2 = (4 * pi^2 / (G * M)) * a^3 — T is the orbital period (5.94×10⁷ s); a is the orbital radius (2.279×10¹¹ m); G is the gravitational constant; M is the Sun's mass (1.989×10³⁰ kg). Each step is a substitution, a rearrangement, or an integration of a stated relation, and the worked solution shows every one. The numerical check then evaluates the final formula for the stated values and obtains T²/a³ = 2.98×10⁻¹⁹ s²/m³, which the demo recomputes in code and compares with the stated value. A new Signature instance of Kepler's third law, checked on the orbit of Mars instead of the public record's Earth. The derivation and the check agree, which is the verification this record offers.

Science Proof

The derivation is scientifically sound because each step follows from a stated principle or a definition, and no step smuggles in the conclusion. A new Signature instance of Kepler's third law, checked on the orbit of Mars instead of the public record's Earth. The final formula is then tested numerically: the demo evaluates it for the stated values and checks it against an independent computation of the same quantity, so an algebraic slip would show up as a failed check. The record states where the derivation's assumptions hold — the idealisations, the ranges, and the regimes where the formula stops applying — so the result is used only inside its domain.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
T = 59400000.0
a = 227900000000.0
G = 6.674e-11
M_sun = 1.989e+30
ratio = T ** 2 / a ** 3
_expected_ratio = 2.98e-19
_check_ratio = abs(ratio - _expected_ratio) <= 1e-21
_chk_ratio_theory = 4.0 * math.pi ** 2 / (G * M_sun)
_exp_ratio_theory = 2.98e-19
_ok_ratio_theory = abs(_chk_ratio_theory - _exp_ratio_theory) <= 1e-21
lines = [
    f"T = {T:.3e} s",
    f"a = {a:.3e} m",
    f"G = {G:.3e} SI",
    f"M_sun = {M_sun:.3e} kg",
    f"ratio = {ratio:.2e} s^2/m^3",
    f"Check ratio against stated value {_expected_ratio:.2e} s^2/m^3: {'PASS' if _check_ratio else 'FAIL'}",
    f"Check ratio_theory: {'PASS' if _ok_ratio_theory else 'FAIL'} (computed {_chk_ratio_theory:.2e}, expected {_exp_ratio_theory:.2e})",
]
payload = f"ratio={ratio:.6g}; ratio_theory={_chk_ratio_theory:.6g}"
for _line in lines:
    print(_line)
_h1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
_h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {_h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {_h2}")
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if _h1 == _h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

Full working program

import hashlib


class PresolvedSolution:
    """Derive Kepler's third law (Signature variant): the orbit of Mars — presolved solution. run() recomputes the answer from the stated inputs."""

    def run(self):
        import math
        T = 59400000.0
        a = 227900000000.0
        G = 6.674e-11
        M_sun = 1.989e+30
        ratio = T ** 2 / a ** 3
        _expected_ratio = 2.98e-19
        _check_ratio = abs(ratio - _expected_ratio) <= 1e-21
        _chk_ratio_theory = 4.0 * math.pi ** 2 / (G * M_sun)
        _exp_ratio_theory = 2.98e-19
        _ok_ratio_theory = abs(_chk_ratio_theory - _exp_ratio_theory) <= 1e-21
        lines = [
            f"T = {T:.3e} s",
            f"a = {a:.3e} m",
            f"G = {G:.3e} SI",
            f"M_sun = {M_sun:.3e} kg",
            f"ratio = {ratio:.2e} s^2/m^3",
            f"Check ratio against stated value {_expected_ratio:.2e} s^2/m^3: {'PASS' if _check_ratio else 'FAIL'}",
            f"Check ratio_theory: {'PASS' if _ok_ratio_theory else 'FAIL'} (computed {_chk_ratio_theory:.2e}, expected {_exp_ratio_theory:.2e})",
        ]
        payload = f"ratio={ratio:.6g}; ratio_theory={_chk_ratio_theory:.6g}"
        return lines, payload


if __name__ == "__main__":
    _solution = PresolvedSolution()
    _lines, _payload = _solution.run()
    for _line in _lines:
        print(_line)
    _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {_h1}")
    print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {_h2}")
    print("Deterministic: MATCH" if _h1 == _h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

The Problem

Derive the pressure variation with depth P = P₀ + ρgh for a static fluid, and check it numerically for water at h = 12 m (P₀ = 101,325 Pa, ρ = 1,000 kg/m³, g = 9.81 m/s²).

The Worked Solution

The solution proceeds in the following steps. Step 1: Consider a column of fluid of height h and cross-sectional area A. Step 2: Its weight is ρ × A × h × g, and pressure is force over area, so the extra pressure at depth h is ρgh. Step 3: Adding the surface pressure P₀ gives P = P₀ + ρgh. Step 4: Numerical check: P = 101,325 + 1,000 × 9.81 × 12 = 101,325 + 117,720 = 219,045 Pa. Step 5: Cross-check: the added pressure ρgh alone is 117,720 Pa, agreeing with the hydrostatic calculation. That completes the worked solution; the answer is P = 219,045 Pa.

The Answer

Derived: P = P₀ + ρgh. Numerical check: at 12 m depth in water, P = 219,045 Pa. That value is the numerical check of the derived formula for the stated inputs; the formula itself is the derivation's answer, and the number shows the formula working on a concrete case.

When It Applies

This record applies whenever the stated problem is the problem in front of you: the same question, the same configuration, the same stated inputs. The solution is checked in three ways. First, the worked solution shows every step, so a reader can follow the arithmetic or the table row by row and find any slip. Second, the demo recomputes the answer from the problem's own inputs in code and prints the result together with a SHA-256 digest of the computed payload, run twice, with a Deterministic: MATCH line confirming the two runs agree; the demo also compares the computed answer with the stated value and prints PASS when they agree. Third, the program repeats the same computation as a reusable routine, so the answer can be regenerated on demand. As a Signature design-study record, the verification is the execution itself: the problem is newly composed, and its solution is verified by the demo's recomputation.

import hashlib
import math
P0 = 101325.0
rho = 1000.0
g = 9.81
h = 12.0
P = P0 + rho * g * h
_expected_P = 219045.0
_check_P = abs(P - _expected_P) <= 1.0
_chk_added_p = rho * g * h
_exp_added_p = 117720.0
_ok_added_p = abs(_chk_added_p - _exp_added_p) <= 1.0
lines = [
    f"P0 = {P0:.0f} Pa",
    f"rho = {rho:.0f} kg/m^3",
    f"g = {g:.2f} m/s^2",
    f"h = {h:.2f} m",
    f"P = {P:.0f} Pa",
    f"Check P against stated value {_expected_P:.0f} Pa: {'PASS' if _check_P else 'FAIL'}",
    f"Check added_p: {'PASS' if _ok_added_p else 'FAIL'} (computed {_chk_added_p:.0f}, expected {_exp_added_p:.0f})",
]
payload = f"P={P:.6g}; added_p={_chk_added_p:.6g}"
for _line in lines:
    print(_line)
_h1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
_h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {_h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {_h2}")
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if _h1 == _h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

class PresolvedSolution:
    """Derive pressure variation with depth (Signature variant): 12 m — presolved solution. run() recomputes the answer from the stated inputs."""

        import math
        P0 = 101325.0
        rho = 1000.0
        g = 9.81
        h = 12.0
        P = P0 + rho * g * h
        _expected_P = 219045.0
        _check_P = abs(P - _expected_P) <= 1.0
        _chk_added_p = rho * g * h
        _exp_added_p = 117720.0
        _ok_added_p = abs(_chk_added_p - _exp_added_p) <= 1.0
        lines = [
            f"P0 = {P0:.0f} Pa",
            f"rho = {rho:.0f} kg/m^3",
            f"g = {g:.2f} m/s^2",
            f"h = {h:.2f} m",
            f"P = {P:.0f} Pa",
            f"Check P against stated value {_expected_P:.0f} Pa: {'PASS' if _check_P else 'FAIL'}",
            f"Check added_p: {'PASS' if _ok_added_p else 'FAIL'} (computed {_chk_added_p:.0f}, expected {_exp_added_p:.0f})",
        ]
        payload = f"P={P:.6g}; added_p={_chk_added_p:.6g}"
        return lines, payload


if __name__ == "__main__":
    _solution = PresolvedSolution()
    _lines, _payload = _solution.run()
    for _line in _lines:
        print(_line)
    _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()

Description

This record presolves the physics derivation 'Derive the work–energy theorem (Signature variant): 3 kg from 2 m/s to 8 m/s'. It starts from the stated first principles, carries the derivation through every algebraic step with nothing skipped, states the final formula, and then checks that formula against a worked numerical example. A derivation is the purest presolved shape: the answer is not a recalled fact but a result proved from principles, and the record keeps the proof and the check side by side so that an AI retrieving it gets both the reasoning and the evidence. The runnable demo re-performs the derivation's numerical check in code, so the final formula is verified by execution as well as by algebra.

The Problem

Derive the work–energy theorem (net work equals the change in kinetic energy), and check it numerically for m = 3 kg accelerating from 2 m/s to 8 m/s.

The Worked Solution

The solution proceeds in the following steps. Step 1: Work is W = F × s. With F = ma and v² = u² + 2as, the distance is s = (v² − u²)/(2a). Step 2: Substituting: W = ma × (v² − u²)/(2a) = ½m(v² − u²). Step 3: But ½mv² is the final kinetic energy and ½mu² is the initial kinetic energy, so W = KE_final − KE_initial. Step 4: Numerical check: W = 0.5 × 3 × (64 − 4) = 90 J. Step 5: Cross-check: KE_final = 96 J and KE_initial = 6 J, a difference of 90 J, agreeing. That completes the worked solution; the answer is W = 90 J.

The Answer

Derived: W_net = ΔKE. Numerical check: for m = 3 kg from 2 m/s to 8 m/s, the net work is 90 J. That value is the numerical check of the derived formula for the stated inputs; the formula itself is the derivation's answer, and the number shows the formula working on a concrete case.

When It Applies

This record applies whenever the stated problem is the problem in front of you: the same question, the same configuration, the same stated inputs. The solution is checked in three ways. First, the worked solution shows every step, so a reader can follow the arithmetic or the table row by row and find any slip. Second, the demo recomputes the answer from the problem's own inputs in code and prints the result together with a SHA-256 digest of the computed payload, run twice, with a Deterministic: MATCH line confirming the two runs agree; the demo also compares the computed answer with the stated value and prints PASS when they agree. Third, the program repeats the same computation as a reusable routine, so the answer can be regenerated on demand. As a Signature design-study record, the verification is the execution itself: the problem is newly composed, and its solution is verified by the demo's recomputation.

Limits

The answer holds only inside its stated conditions. The derivation holds only under its stated assumptions: the idealisations named in the solution (constant acceleration, no friction, point masses, ideal gases, and similar) limit where the result applies, and outside those conditions the formula must be corrected or replaced. Beyond those conditions the answer changes or stops applying: different inputs give different answers, and the record's numbers must not be carried into a different configuration without redoing the work. Nothing here is a design approval, a safety certification, or a claim about a physical system that was never measured. The record proves what it states about the stated problem and no more, and any use beyond that problem should repeat the computation with the new data.

Math Proof

The mathematical justification is the chain of algebra from the starting principle to the final formula. W = 0.5 * m * (v^2 - u^2) — W is the net work (J); m is the mass (3 kg); v is the final speed (8 m/s); u is the initial speed (2 m/s). Each step is a substitution, a rearrangement, or an integration of a stated relation, and the worked solution shows every one. The numerical check then evaluates the final formula for the stated values and obtains W = 90 J, which the demo recomputes in code and compares with the stated value. A new Signature instance of the work-energy theorem, with a heavier mass and a larger speed change than the public record. The derivation and the check agree, which is the verification this record offers.

Science Proof

The derivation is scientifically sound because each step follows from a stated principle or a definition, and no step smuggles in the conclusion. A new Signature instance of the work-energy theorem, with a heavier mass and a larger speed change than the public record. The final formula is then tested numerically: the demo evaluates it for the stated values and checks it against an independent computation of the same quantity, so an algebraic slip would show up as a failed check. The record states where the derivation's assumptions hold — the idealisations, the ranges, and the regimes where the formula stops applying — so the result is used only inside its domain.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
m = 3.0
u = 2.0
v = 8.0
W = 0.5 * m * (v ** 2 - u ** 2)
_expected_W = 90.0
_check_W = abs(W - _expected_W) <= 0.01
_chk_KE_diff = 0.5 * m * v ** 2 - 0.5 * m * u ** 2
_exp_KE_diff = 90.0
_ok_KE_diff = abs(_chk_KE_diff - _exp_KE_diff) <= 0.01
lines = [
    f"m = {m:.2f} kg",
    f"u = {u:.2f} m/s",
    f"v = {v:.2f} m/s",
    f"W = {W:.2f} J",
    f"Check W against stated value {_expected_W:.2f} J: {'PASS' if _check_W else 'FAIL'}",
    f"Check KE_diff: {'PASS' if _ok_KE_diff else 'FAIL'} (computed {_chk_KE_diff:.2f}, expected {_exp_KE_diff:.2f})",
]
payload = f"W={W:.6g}; KE_diff={_chk_KE_diff:.6g}"
for _line in lines:
    print(_line)
_h1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
_h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {_h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {_h2}")
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if _h1 == _h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

Full working program

import hashlib


class PresolvedSolution:
    """Derive the work–energy theorem (Signature variant): 3 kg from 2 m/s to 8 m/s — presolved solution. run() recomputes the answer from the stated inputs."""

    def run(self):
        import math
        m = 3.0
        u = 2.0
        v = 8.0
        W = 0.5 * m * (v ** 2 - u ** 2)
        _expected_W = 90.0
        _check_W = abs(W - _expected_W) <= 0.01
        _chk_KE_diff = 0.5 * m * v ** 2 - 0.5 * m * u ** 2
        _exp_KE_diff = 90.0
        _ok_KE_diff = abs(_chk_KE_diff - _exp_KE_diff) <= 0.01
        lines = [
            f"m = {m:.2f} kg",
            f"u = {u:.2f} m/s",
            f"v = {v:.2f} m/s",
            f"W = {W:.2f} J",
            f"Check W against stated value {_expected_W:.2f} J: {'PASS' if _check_W else 'FAIL'}",
            f"Check KE_diff: {'PASS' if _ok_KE_diff else 'FAIL'} (computed {_chk_KE_diff:.2f}, expected {_exp_KE_diff:.2f})",
        ]
        payload = f"W={W:.6g}; KE_diff={_chk_KE_diff:.6g}"
        return lines, payload


if __name__ == "__main__":
    _solution = PresolvedSolution()
    _lines, _payload = _solution.run()
    for _line in _lines:
        print(_line)
    _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {_h1}")
    print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {_h2}")
    print("Deterministic: MATCH" if _h1 == _h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

The Problem

Derive conservation of momentum from Newton's third law, and check it numerically for a perfectly inelastic collision: m1 = 4 kg at 5 m/s strikes m2 = 6 kg at rest, and they stick together.

The Worked Solution

The solution proceeds in the following steps. Step 1: Newton's third law says the force on body 1 from body 2 is equal and opposite to the force on body 2 from body 1. Step 2: Over the collision time Δt, the impulses are therefore equal and opposite: Δp1 = −Δp2. Step 3: So the total momentum change is zero: p1 + p2 is the same before and after. Step 4: Numerical check: before, p = 4 × 5 + 6 × 0 = 20 kg·m/s. After, the combined mass is 10 kg, so v' = 20/10 = 2.0 m/s and p = 10 × 2.0 = 20 kg·m/s. Step 5: Momentum is conserved; kinetic energy is not (this collision is inelastic). That completes the worked solution; the answer is p = 20 kg·m/s before and after; v' = 2.0 m/s.

The Answer

Derived: total momentum is conserved in a collision. Numerical check: momentum before is 20 kg·m/s and after is 20 kg·m/s, with a common final speed of 2.0 m/s. That value is the numerical check of the derived formula for the stated inputs; the formula itself is the derivation's answer, and the number shows the formula working on a concrete case.

import hashlib


class PresolvedSolution:
    """Derive conservation of momentum (Signature variant): 4 kg at 5 m/s meets 6 kg at rest — presolved solution. run() recomputes the answer from the stated inputs."""

    def run(self):
        import math
        m1 = 4.0
        v1 = 5.0
        m2 = 6.0
        v2 = 0.0
        p_before = m1 * v1 + m2 * v2
        _expected_p_before = 20.0
        _check_p_before = abs(p_before - _expected_p_before) <= 0.01
        v_final = p_before / (m1 + m2)
        _expected_v_final = 2.0
        _check_v_final = abs(v_final - _expected_v_final) <= 0.01
        _chk_p_after = (m1 + m2) * v_final
        _exp_p_after = 20.0
        _ok_p_after = abs(_chk_p_after - _exp_p_after) <= 0.01
        lines = [
            f"m1 = {m1:.2f} kg",
            f"v1 = {v1:.2f} m/s",
            f"m2 = {m2:.2f} kg",
            f"v2 = {v2:.2f} m/s",
            f"p_before = {p_before:.2f} kg m/s",
            f"Check p_before against stated value {_expected_p_before:.2f} kg m/s: {'PASS' if _check_p_before else 'FAIL'}",
            f"v_final = {v_final:.2f} m/s",
            f"Check v_final against stated value {_expected_v_final:.2f} m/s: {'PASS' if _check_v_final else 'FAIL'}",
            f"Check p_after: {'PASS' if _ok_p_after else 'FAIL'} (computed {_chk_p_after:.2f}, expected {_exp_p_after:.2f})",
        ]
        payload = f"p_before={p_before:.6g}; v_final={v_final:.6g}; p_after={_chk_p_after:.6g}"
        return lines, payload


if __name__ == "__main__":
    _solution = PresolvedSolution()

History

Description

This record presolves the physics derivation 'Derive Snell's law (Signature variant): water to air at 40°'. It starts from the stated first principles, carries the derivation through every algebraic step with nothing skipped, states the final formula, and then checks that formula against a worked numerical example. A derivation is the purest presolved shape: the answer is not a recalled fact but a result proved from principles, and the record keeps the proof and the check side by side so that an AI retrieving it gets both the reasoning and the evidence. The runnable demo re-performs the derivation's numerical check in code, so the final formula is verified by execution as well as by algebra.

The Problem

Derive Snell's law from Fermat's principle of least time, and check it numerically for light passing from water (n1 = 1.33) into air (n2 = 1) at an incidence angle of 40°.

The Worked Solution

The solution proceeds in the following steps. Step 1: Fermat's principle: light takes the path of least travel time between two points. Step 2: For a ray crossing a flat interface, the travel time is a function of the crossing point; minimising it with respect to that point gives the condition n1×sin(θ1) = n2×sin(θ2). Step 3: That condition is Snell's law: the sines of the angles are in inverse ratio to the refractive indices. Step 4: Numerical check: sin(θ2) = (1.33/1) × sin(40°) = 1.33 × 0.6428 = 0.8549. Step 5: So θ2 = arcsin(0.8549) = 58.75°, bending away from the normal as expected going into the less dense medium. That completes the worked solution; the answer is θ2 = 58.75°.

The Answer

Derived: n1×sin(θ1) = n2×sin(θ2). Numerical check: from water into air at 40° incidence, the refraction angle is 58.75°. That value is the numerical check of the derived formula for the stated inputs; the formula itself is the derivation's answer, and the number shows the formula working on a concrete case.

When It Applies

This record applies whenever the stated problem is the problem in front of you: the same question, the same configuration, the same stated inputs. The solution is checked in three ways. First, the worked solution shows every step, so a reader can follow the arithmetic or the table row by row and find any slip. Second, the demo recomputes the answer from the problem's own inputs in code and prints the result together with a SHA-256 digest of the computed payload, run twice, with a Deterministic: MATCH line confirming the two runs agree; the demo also compares the computed answer with the stated value and prints PASS when they agree. Third, the program repeats the same computation as a reusable routine, so the answer can be regenerated on demand. As a Signature design-study record, the verification is the execution itself: the problem is newly composed, and its solution is verified by the demo's recomputation.

Limits

The answer holds only inside its stated conditions. The derivation holds only under its stated assumptions: the idealisations named in the solution (constant acceleration, no friction, point masses, ideal gases, and similar) limit where the result applies, and outside those conditions the formula must be corrected or replaced. Beyond those conditions the answer changes or stops applying: different inputs give different answers, and the record's numbers must not be carried into a different configuration without redoing the work. Nothing here is a design approval, a safety certification, or a claim about a physical system that was never measured. The record proves what it states about the stated problem and no more, and any use beyond that problem should repeat the computation with the new data.

Math Proof

The mathematical justification is the chain of algebra from the starting principle to the final formula. n1 * sin(theta1) = n2 * sin(theta2) — n1 and n2 are the refractive indices (1.33 and 1); theta1 is the incidence angle (40°); theta2 is the refraction angle (58.75°). Each step is a substitution, a rearrangement, or an integration of a stated relation, and the worked solution shows every one. The numerical check then evaluates the final formula for the stated values and obtains θ2 = 58.75°, which the demo recomputes in code and compares with the stated value. import hashlib
import math
n1 = 1.33
n2 = 1.0
theta1 = 40.0
sin_theta2 = n1 / n2 * math.sin(math.radians(theta1))
_expected_sin_theta2 = 0.8549
_check_sin_theta2 = abs(sin_theta2 - _expected_sin_theta2) <= 0.001
theta2 = math.degrees(math.asin(sin_theta2))
_expected_theta2 = 58.75
_check_theta2 = abs(theta2 - _expected_theta2) <= 0.01
_chk_snell_lhs = n1 * math.sin(math.radians(theta1))
_exp_snell_lhs = 0.8549
_ok_snell_lhs = abs(_chk_snell_lhs - _exp_snell_lhs) <= 0.001
lines = [
    f"n1 = {n1:.2f} ",
    f"n2 = {n2:.2f} ",
    f"theta1 = {theta1:.2f} deg",
    f"sin_theta2 = {sin_theta2:.4f} ",
    f"Check sin_theta2 against stated value {_expected_sin_theta2:.4f} : {'PASS' if _check_sin_theta2 else 'FAIL'}",
    f"theta2 = {theta2:.2f} deg",
    f"Check theta2 against stated value {_expected_theta2:.2f} deg: {'PASS' if _check_theta2 else 'FAIL'}",
    f"Check snell_lhs: {'PASS' if _ok_snell_lhs else 'FAIL'} (computed {_chk_snell_lhs:.4f}, expected {_exp_snell_lhs:.4f})",
]
payload = f"sin_theta2={sin_theta2:.6g}; theta2={theta2:.6g}; snell_lhs={_chk_snell_lhs:.6g}"
for _line in lines:
    print(_line)
_h1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
_h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {_h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {_h2}")
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if _h1 == _h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

The Problem

Derive the thin-lens equation 1/f = 1/do + 1/di from ray geometry, and check it numerically for a lens of focal length f = 15 cm with an object at do = 45 cm.

The Worked Solution

The solution proceeds in the following steps. Step 1: Trace two rays from the top of the object: one through the centre (undeflected) and one parallel to the axis (refracted through the focal point). Step 2: Similar triangles from the object, the lens, and the image give the magnification relations h_i/h_o = d_i/d_o and h_i/h_o = (d_i − f)/f. Step 3: Equating the two expressions for the magnification gives d_i/d_o = (d_i − f)/f. Step 4: Cross-multiplying and dividing by f×d_o×d_i gives 1/f = 1/d_o + 1/d_i. Step 5: Numerical check: 1/d_i = 1/15 − 1/45 = 0.0444 per cm, so d_i = 22.5 cm. That completes the worked solution; the answer is di = 22.5 cm.

The Answer

Derived: 1/f = 1/do + 1/di. Numerical check: with f = 15 cm and do = 45 cm, the image forms at di = 22.5 cm. That value is the numerical check of the derived formula for the stated inputs; the formula itself is the derivation's answer, and the number shows the formula working on a concrete case.

History

Derivations of this form are the backbone of classical and modern physics as taught in the standard university texts, above all the OpenStax University Physics sequence and the treatments collected in HyperPhysics. Each one begins from a definition or a conservation law and ends at a formula that working physicists and engineers use daily. This instance, 'Derive the lens equation (Signature variant): f = 15 cm, object at 45 cm', is derived here from first principles with fixed numerical values for the check, so that every step can be followed and the final formula tested against a concrete case.

Problem

Derive the thin-lens equation 1/f = 1/do + 1/di from ray geometry, and check it numerically for a lens of focal length f = 15 cm with an object at do = 45 cm.

Method

The solution proceeds in the following steps. Step 1: Trace two rays from the top of the object: one through the centre (undeflected) and one parallel to the axis (refracted through the focal point). Step 2: Similar triangles from the object, the lens, and the image give the magnification relations h_i/h_o = d_i/d_o and h_i/h_o = (d_i − f)/f. Step 3: Equating the two expressions for the magnification gives d_i/d_o = (d_i − f)/f. Step 4: Cross-multiplying and dividing by f×d_o×d_i gives 1/f = 1/d_o + 1/d_i. Step 5: Numerical check: 1/d_i = 1/15 − 1/45 = 0.0444 per cm, so d_i = 22.5 cm. That completes the worked solution; the answer is di = 22.5 cm.

Dataset

Stated numerical values for the check (see key figures); physical constants (g, G, R, h, k, epsilon_0) from the standard references listed in sources.

import hashlib
import math
f = 15.0
do = 45.0
di = 1.0 / (1.0 / f - 1.0 / do)
_expected_di = 22.5
_check_di = abs(di - _expected_di) <= 0.01
_chk_lens_lhs = 1.0 / f
_exp_lens_lhs = 0.0667
_ok_lens_lhs = abs(_chk_lens_lhs - _exp_lens_lhs) <= 0.001
lines = [
    f"f = {f:.2f} cm",
    f"do = {do:.2f} cm",
    f"di = {di:.2f} cm",
    f"Check di against stated value {_expected_di:.2f} cm: {'PASS' if _check_di else 'FAIL'}",
    f"Check lens_lhs: {'PASS' if _ok_lens_lhs else 'FAIL'} (computed {_chk_lens_lhs:.4f}, expected {_exp_lens_lhs:.4f})",
]
payload = f"di={di:.6g}; lens_lhs={_chk_lens_lhs:.6g}"
for _line in lines:
    print(_line)
_h1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
_h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {_h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {_h2}")
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if _h1 == _h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

The Problem

Derive the Doppler shift formula for sound and check it numerically for a 440 Hz source receding from a stationary observer at 15 m/s (speed of sound 343 m/s).

The Worked Solution

The solution proceeds in the following steps. Step 1: A source moving away from the observer at speed v_s emits each crest from a point farther than the last, stretching the wavelength. Step 2: In one period the source retreats v_s×T, so the observed wavelength is λ' = λ + v_s×T = (v + v_s)/f. Step 3: The observed frequency is f' = v/λ' = f × v/(v + v_s). Step 4: Numerical check: f' = 440 × 343/(343 + 15) = 440 × 0.9581 = 421.56 Hz. Step 5: The pitch drops by about 18 Hz as the source recedes. That completes the worked solution; the answer is f' = 421.56 Hz.

The Answer

Derived: f' = f×v/(v + v_s) for a receding source. Numerical check: a 440 Hz source receding at 15 m/s is heard at 421.56 Hz. That value is the numerical check of the derived formula for the stated inputs; the formula itself is the derivation's answer, and the number shows the formula working on a concrete case.

The Problem

Derive the ideal gas law PV = nRT from kinetic theory, and check it numerically for n = 2 mol of gas at T = 300 K in V = 0.05 m³ (R = 8.314 J/(mol·K)).

The Worked Solution

The solution proceeds in the following steps. Step 1: Kinetic theory: gas molecules of mass m and mean square speed <c²> strike the walls; the pressure is P = (1/3)×(N/V)×m×<c²>. Step 2: The mean kinetic energy per molecule is (1/2)m<c²> = (3/2)kT, where k is Boltzmann's constant and T the absolute temperature. Step 3: Substituting gives P = NkT/V, and with N = n×N_A and R = N_A×k, this is PV = nRT. Step 4: Numerical check: P = 2 × 8.314 × 300 / 0.05 = 99,768 Pa. Step 5: That is just under one atmosphere, as expected for two moles in a larger volume at a warmer temperature. That completes the worked solution; the answer is P = 99,768 Pa.

The Answer

Derived: PV = nRT. Numerical check: two moles at 300 K in 0.05 m³ give P = 99,768 Pa. That value is the numerical check of the derived formula for the stated inputs; the formula itself is the derivation's answer, and the number shows the formula working on a concrete case.

When It Applies

This record applies whenever the stated problem is the problem in front of you: the same question, the same configuration, the same stated inputs. The solution is checked in three ways. First, the worked solution shows every step, so a reader can follow the arithmetic or the table row by row and find any slip. History

Derivations of this form are the backbone of classical and modern physics as taught in the standard university texts, above all the OpenStax University Physics sequence and the treatments collected in HyperPhysics. Each one begins from a definition or a conservation law and ends at a formula that working physicists and engineers use daily. This instance, 'Derive the ideal gas law (Signature variant): 2 mol at 300 K in 0.05 m³', is derived here from first principles with fixed numerical values for the check, so that every step can be followed and the final formula tested against a concrete case.

Problem

Derive the ideal gas law PV = nRT from kinetic theory, and check it numerically for n = 2 mol of gas at T = 300 K in V = 0.05 m³ (R = 8.314 J/(mol·K)).

Method

The solution proceeds in the following steps. Step 1: Kinetic theory: gas molecules of mass m and mean square speed <c²> strike the walls; the pressure is P = (1/3)×(N/V)×m×<c²>. Step 2: The mean kinetic energy per molecule is (1/2)m<c²> = (3/2)kT, where k is Boltzmann's constant and T the absolute temperature. Step 3: Substituting gives P = NkT/V, and with N = n×N_A and R = N_A×k, this is PV = nRT. Step 4: Numerical check: P = 2 × 8.314 × 300 / 0.05 = 99,768 Pa. Step 5: That is just under one atmosphere, as expected for two moles in a larger volume at a warmer temperature. That completes the worked solution; the answer is P = 99,768 Pa.

Stated numerical values for the check (see key figures); physical constants (g, G, R, h, k, epsilon_0) from the standard references listed in sources.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
n = 2.0
R = 8.314
T = 300.0
V = 0.05
P = n * R * T / V
_expected_P = 99768.0
_check_P = abs(P - _expected_P) <= 1.0
lines = [
    f"n = {n:.2f} mol",
    f"R = {R:.3f} J/(mol K)",
    f"T = {T:.2f} K",
    f"V = {V:.3f} m^3",
    f"P = {P:.0f} Pa",
    f"Check P against stated value {_expected_P:.0f} Pa: {'PASS' if _check_P else 'FAIL'}",
]
payload = f"P={P:.6g}"
for _line in lines:
    print(_line)
_h1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
_h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {_h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {_h2}")
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if _h1 == _h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

Full working program

import hashlib


class PresolvedSolution:
    """Derive the ideal gas law (Signature variant): 2 mol at 300 K in 0.05 m³ — presolved solution. run() recomputes the answer from the stated inputs."""

    def run(self):
        import math
        n = 2.0
        R = 8.314
        T = 300.0
        V = 0.05
        P = n * R * T / V
        _expected_P = 99768.0
        _check_P = abs(P - _expected_P) <= 1.0
        lines = [
            f"n = {n:.2f} mol",
            f"R = {R:.3f} J/(mol K)",
            f"T = {T:.2f} K",
            f"V = {V:.3f} m^3",
            f"P = {P:.0f} Pa",
            f"Check P against stated value {_expected_P:.0f} Pa: {'PASS' if _check_P else 'FAIL'}",
        ]
        payload = f"P={P:.6g}"
        return lines, payload


if __name__ == "__main__":
    _solution = PresolvedSolution()
    _lines, _payload = _solution.run()
    for _line in _lines:
        print(_line)
    _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {_h1}")
    print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {_h2}")
    print("Deterministic: MATCH" if _h1 == _h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

The Problem

Derive the de Broglie wavelength λ = h/p and check it numerically for an electron (m = 9.109×10⁻³¹ kg) moving at v = 2×10⁶ m/s (h = 6.626×10⁻³⁴ J·s).

The Worked Solution

The solution proceeds in the following steps. Step 1: De Broglie proposed that matter has a wavelength given by the same relation as light: λ = h/p, where p is the momentum. Step 2: For a non-relativistic particle the momentum is p = mv. Step 3: Combining gives λ = h/(mv). Step 4: Numerical check: p = 9.109×10⁻³¹ × 2×10⁶ = 1.822×10⁻²⁴ kg·m/s. Step 5: So λ = 6.626×10⁻³⁴ / 1.822×10⁻²⁴ = 3.64×10⁻¹⁰ m. That completes the worked solution; the answer is λ = 3.64×10⁻¹⁰ m.

The Answer

Derived: λ = h/p. Numerical check: an electron at 2×10⁶ m/s has λ = 3.64×10⁻¹⁰ m. That value is the numerical check of the derived formula for the stated inputs; the formula itself is the derivation's answer, and the number shows the formula working on a concrete case.

This record applies whenever the stated problem is the problem in front of you: the same question, the same configuration, the same stated inputs. The solution is checked in three ways. First, the worked solution shows every step, so a reader can follow the arithmetic or the table row by row and find any slip. Second, the demo recomputes the answer from the problem's own inputs in code and prints the result together with a SHA-256 digest of the computed payload, run twice, with a Deterministic: MATCH line confirming the two runs agree; the demo also compares the computed answer with the stated value and prints PASS when they agree. Third, the program repeats the same computation as a reusable routine, so the answer can be regenerated on demand. As a Signature design-study record, the verification is the execution itself: the problem is newly composed, and its solution is verified by the demo's recomputation.

Beyond those conditions the answer changes or stops applying: different inputs give different answers, and the record's numbers must not be carried into a different configuration without redoing the work. Nothing here is a design approval, a safety certification, or a claim about a physical system that was never measured. The mathematical justification is the chain of algebra from the starting principle to the final formula. lambda = h / p, p = m * v — lambda is the de Broglie wavelength (m); h is Planck's constant (6.626×10⁻³⁴ J·s); p is the momentum (kg·m/s); m is the electron mass (9.109×10⁻³¹ kg); v is the speed (2×10⁶ m/s).         return lines, payload

The runnable demo re-performs the derivation's numerical check in code, so the final formula is verified by execution as well as by algebra.

The Problem

Derive the time-dilation factor from the light clock and check it numerically for a clock moving at v = 0.8c.

The Worked Solution

The solution proceeds in the following steps. Step 1: A light clock ticks when a light pulse travels up and down between two mirrors a distance L apart; at rest the round trip takes Δt0 = 2L/c. Step 2: For a clock moving at speed v, the light follows a diagonal path: by Pythagoras, the half-trip length is √(L² + (vΔt/2)²). Step 3: The moving round-trip time is Δt = 2×√(L² + (vΔt/2)²)/c; solving for Δt gives Δt = Δt0/√(1 − v²/c²). Step 4: The factor γ = 1/√(1 − v²/c²) is the time-dilation factor. Step 5: Numerical check: at v = 0.8c, γ = 1/√(1 − 0.64) = 1/0.6 = 1.6667, so a moving clock runs about 1.67 times slower. That completes the worked solution; the answer is γ = 1.6667.

The Answer

Derived: Δt = γΔt0 with γ = 1/√(1 − v²/c²). Numerical check: at v = 0.8c, γ = 1.6667. That value is the numerical check of the derived formula for the stated inputs; the formula itself is the derivation's answer, and the number shows the formula working on a concrete case.

When It Applies

This record applies whenever the stated problem is the problem in front of you: the same question, the same configuration, the same stated inputs. The solution is checked in three ways. First, the worked solution shows every step, so a reader can follow the arithmetic or the table row by row and find any slip. Second, the demo recomputes the answer from the problem's own inputs in code and prints the result together with a SHA-256 digest of the computed payload, run twice, with a Deterministic: MATCH line confirming the two runs agree; the demo also compares the computed answer with the stated value and prints PASS when they agree. Third, the program repeats the same computation as a reusable routine, so the answer can be regenerated on demand. As a Signature design-study record, the verification is the execution itself: the problem is newly composed, and its solution is verified by the demo's recomputation.

Limits

The answer holds only inside its stated conditions. The derivation holds only under its stated assumptions: the idealisations named in the solution (constant acceleration, no friction, point masses, ideal gases, and similar) limit where the result applies, and outside those conditions the formula must be corrected or replaced. Beyond those conditions the answer changes or stops applying: different inputs give different answers, and the record's numbers must not be carried into a different configuration without redoing the work. Nothing here is a design approval, a safety certification, or a claim about a physical system that was never measured. The record proves what it states about the stated problem and no more, and any use beyond that problem should repeat the computation with the new data.

Math Proof

The mathematical justification is the chain of algebra from the starting principle to the final formula. gamma = 1 / sqrt(1 - v^2 / c^2) — gamma is the Lorentz factor; v is the clock's speed (0.8c); c is the speed of light; Δt is the dilated time and Δt0 the proper time. Each step is a substitution, a rearrangement, or an integration of a stated relation, and the worked solution shows every one. The numerical check then evaluates the final formula for the stated values and obtains γ = 1.6667, which the demo recomputes in code and compares with the stated value. A new Signature instance of the time-dilation derivation, at a higher speed than the public record. The derivation and the check agree, which is the verification this record offers.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
beta = 0.8
gamma = 1.0 / math.sqrt(1.0 - beta ** 2)
_expected_gamma = 1.6667
_check_gamma = abs(gamma - _expected_gamma) <= 0.001
_chk_gamma_sq_check = gamma ** 2 * (1.0 - beta ** 2)
_exp_gamma_sq_check = 1.0
_ok_gamma_sq_check = abs(_chk_gamma_sq_check - _exp_gamma_sq_check) <= 0.001
lines = [
    f"beta = {beta:.2f} v/c",
    f"gamma = {gamma:.4f} ",
    f"Check gamma against stated value {_expected_gamma:.4f} : {'PASS' if _check_gamma else 'FAIL'}",
    f"Check gamma_sq_check: {'PASS' if _ok_gamma_sq_check else 'FAIL'} (computed {_chk_gamma_sq_check:.4f}, expected {_exp_gamma_sq_check:.4f})",
]
payload = f"gamma={gamma:.6g}; gamma_sq_check={_chk_gamma_sq_check:.6g}"
for _line in lines:
    print(_line)
_h1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
_h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {_h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {_h2}")
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if _h1 == _h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")
